Several [experimental treatments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinical_research) are being actively studied in [clinical trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinical_trial).[^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_BMJ-275] Others were thought to be promising early in the pandemic, such as [hydroxychloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxychloroquine) and [lopinavir/ritonavir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lopinavir/ritonavir), but later research found them to be ineffective or even harmful,[^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_BMJ-275][^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_Kim_Read_Fauci_p=2149-276][^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_NIHGuidelinesTherapeuticManagement-277] like [fluvoxamine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluvoxamine), a cheap and widely available [antidepressant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antidepressant);[^cite_note-278] In December 2020, two [monoclonal antibody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoclonal_antibody)-based therapies were available in the United States, for early use in cases thought to be at high risk of progression to severe disease.[^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_NIHGuidelinesTherapeuticManagement-277] The antiviral [remdesivir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remdesivir) has been available in the U.S., Canada, Australia, and several other countries, with varying restrictions; it is not recommended for people needing mechanical ventilation and has been discouraged altogether by the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO),[^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_Hsu_p=m4457-279] due to limited evidence of its efficacy.[^cite_note-Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19_BMJ-275]

## Prognosis and risk factors

See also: [COVID-19 pandemic death rates by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_death_rates_by_country)



Medical professionals treating a COVID-19 patient in critical condition in an [intensive care unit](6332859.md) in May 2020.

The severity of COVID‑19 varies. The disease may take a mild course with few or no symptoms, resembling other common upper respiratory diseases such as the [common cold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_cold). In 3–4% of cases (7.4% for those over age 65) symptoms are severe enough to cause hospitalisation.[^cite_note-pmid33087398-280] Mild cases typically recover within two weeks, while those with severe or critical diseases may take three to six weeks to recover. Among those who have died, the time from symptom onset to death has ranged from two to eight weeks.[^cite_note-WHOReport24Feb2020-107] The Italian [Istituto Superiore di Sanità](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istituto_Superiore_di_Sanit%C3%A0) reported that the median time between the onset of symptoms and death was twelve days, with seven being hospitalised. However, people transferred to an ICU had a median time of ten days between hospitalisation and death.[^cite_note-ISSCharacteristics-281] Abnormal sodium levels during hospitalisation with COVID-19 are associated with poor prognoses: high sodium with a greater risk of death, and low sodium with an increased chance of needing ventilator support.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283] Prolonged [prothrombin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prothrombin) time and elevated [C-reactive protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-reactive_protein) levels on admission to the hospital are associated with severe course of COVID‑19 and with a transfer to ICU.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285]

Some early studies suggest 10% to 20% of people with COVID‑19 will experience [symptoms lasting longer than a month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID).[^cite_note-NIHRreportSep20-286][^cite_note-HvIJa-287] A majority of those who were admitted to hospital with severe disease report long-term problems including fatigue and shortness of breath.[^cite_note-UniWashingtonSep20-288] On 30 October 2020, WHO chief [Tedros Adhanom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tedros_Adhanom) warned that "to a significant number of people, the COVID virus poses a range of serious long-term effects". He has described the vast spectrum of COVID‑19 symptoms that fluctuate over time as "really concerning". They range from fatigue, a cough and shortness of breath, to inflammation and injury of major organs – including the lungs and heart, and also neurological and psychological effects. Symptoms often overlap and can affect any system in the body. Infected people have reported cyclical bouts of fatigue, headaches, months of complete exhaustion, mood swings, and other symptoms. Tedros therefore concluded that a strategy of achieving [herd immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herd_immunity) by infection, rather than vaccination, is "morally unconscionable and unfeasible".[^cite_note-OaTsI-289]

In terms of hospital readmissions about 9% of 106,000 individuals had to return for hospital treatment within two months of discharge. The average to readmit was eight days since first hospital visit. There are several risk factors that have been identified as being a cause of multiple admissions to a hospital facility. Among these are advanced age (above 65 years of age) and presence of a chronic condition such as diabetes, COPD, heart failure or chronic kidney disease.[^cite_note-rArHO-290][^cite_note-CtbMg-291]

According to [scientific reviews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_review) smokers are more likely to require intensive care or die compared to non-smokers.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-engin-review-293] Acting on the same ACE2 pulmonary receptors affected by smoking, air pollution has been correlated with the disease.[^cite_note-engin-review-293] Short-term[^cite_note-294] and chronic[^cite_note-295] exposure to air pollution seems to enhance morbidity and mortality from COVID‑19.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] Pre-existing heart and lung diseases[^cite_note-299] and also [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity), especially in conjunction with [fatty liver disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatty_liver_disease), contributes to an increased health risk of COVID‑19.[^cite_note-engin-review-293][^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302]

It is also assumed that those that are immunocompromised are at higher risk of getting severely sick from SARS-CoV-2.[^cite_note-303] One research study that looked into the COVID‑19 infections in hospitalised kidney transplant recipients found a mortality rate of 11%.[^cite_note-304]

Men with untreated [hypogonadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypogonadism) were 2.4 times more likely than men with eugonadism to be hospitalised if they contracted COVID-19; Hypogonad men treated with [testosterone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testosterone) were less likely to be hospitalised for COVID-19 than men who were not treated for hypogonadism.[^cite_note-jama-305]

### Genetic risk factors

[Genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics) plays an important role in the ability to fight off Covid.[^cite_note-pmid33888907-306] For instance, those that do not produce detectable [type I interferons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_I_interferon) or produce [auto-antibodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autoantibody) against these may get much sicker from COVID‑19.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] [Genetic screening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_screening) is able to detect [interferon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interferon) effector genes.[^cite_note-309] Some genetic variants are risk factors in specific populations. For instance, an [allele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allele) of the [DOCK2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DOCK2) gene (dedicator of cytokinesis 2 gene) is a common risk factor in Asian populations but much less common in Europe. The mutation leads to lower expression of DOCK2 especially in younger people with severe COVID-19 infections.[^cite_note-310] In fact, many other genes and genetic variants have been found that determine the outcome of SARS-CoV-2 infections.[^cite_note-311]

#### Blood group

This section is an excerpt from [Relationship between blood group and COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relationship_between_blood_group_and_COVID-19).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Relationship_between_blood_group_and_COVID-19\&action=edit)]

A series of studies have examined a potential [relationship between blood group and COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relationship_between_blood_group_and_COVID-19). Initial research conducted by scientists in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan), [China](5405.md), on thousands of infected patients suggested that individuals with [blood group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_type) A were more likely to experience severe symptoms of COVID-19 infection compared to those with blood group O.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313] Other blood types fell between these two in terms of relative risk.

A second study, published in the *[New England Journal of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England_Journal_of_Medicine)*, did not establish a [causal relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Causal_relationship) between blood type and the severity of COVID-19, but it did support the earlier findings from Chinese researchers.[^cite_note-Relationship_between_blood_group_and_COVID-19_hamshahrionline.ir-314] However, later studies conducted in other countries did not confirm a significant or meaningful link between blood type and disease severity, calling into question the universality of the initial findings.

### Children

See also: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_children)

While very young children have experienced lower rates of infection, older children have a rate of infection that is similar to the population as a whole.[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316] Children are likely to have milder symptoms and are at lower risk of severe disease than adults.[^cite_note-Reardon-317] The CDC reports that in the US roughly a third of hospitalised children were admitted to the ICU,[^cite_note-318] while a European multinational study of hospitalised children from June 2020, found that about 8% of children admitted to a hospital needed intensive care.[^cite_note-319] Four of the 582 children (0.7%) in the European study died, but the actual mortality rate may be "substantially lower" since milder cases that did not seek medical help were not included in the study.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-CDC_2020children-321]

### Long-term effects

Further information: [Long COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID)

Around 10% to 30% of non-hospitalised people with COVID-19 go on to develop [long COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID). For those that do need hospitalisation, the incidence of long-term effects is over 50%.[^cite_note-davis-15] Long COVID is an often severe multisystem disease with a large set of symptoms. Common symptoms are [fatigue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatigue), [post-exertional malaise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-exertional_malaise), [cognitive issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_impairment), [shortness of breath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortness_of_breath) and pain. There are likely various, possibly coinciding, causes.[^cite_note-davis-15] Organ damage from the acute infection can explain a part of the symptoms, but long COVID is also observed in people where organ damage seems to be absent.[^cite_note-pmid35594336-322]

By a variety of mechanisms, the lungs are the organs most affected in COVID‑19.[^cite_note-Torres-323] In people requiring hospital admission, up to 98% of CT scans performed show lung abnormalities after 28 days of illness even if they had clinically improved.[^cite_note-324] People with advanced age, severe disease, prolonged ICU stays, or who smoke are more likely to have long-lasting effects, including pulmonary fibrosis.[^cite_note-Rai-325] Overall, approximately one-third of those investigated after four weeks will have findings of [pulmonary fibrosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulmonary_fibrosis) or reduced lung function as measured by [DLCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DLCO), even in asymptomatic people, but with the suggestion of continuing improvement with the passing of more time.[^cite_note-Torres-323] After severe disease, lung function can take anywhere from three months to a year or more to return to previous levels.[^cite_note-326]

The risks of [cognitive deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_deficit), [dementia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dementia), psychotic disorders, and [epilepsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epilepsy) or seizures persists at an increased level two years after infection.[^cite_note-327]

### Immunity

See also: [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md)



Human [antibody response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibody_response) to SARS-CoV-2 infection

The [immune response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immune_response) by humans to SARS-CoV-2 virus occurs as a combination of the [cell-mediated immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell-mediated_immunity) and antibody production,[^cite_note-328] just as with most other infections.[^cite_note-329] B cells interact with T cells and begin dividing before selection into the plasma cell, partly on the basis of their affinity for antigen.[^cite_note-330] Since SARS-CoV-2 has been in the human population only since December 2019, it remains unknown if the [immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunity_\(medical\)) is long-lasting in people who recover from the disease.[^cite_note-CohenJI2020Dec-331] The presence of neutralising antibodies in blood strongly correlates with protection from infection, but the level of neutralising antibody declines with time. Those with asymptomatic or mild disease had undetectable levels of neutralising antibody two months after infection. In another study, the level of neutralising antibodies fell four-fold one to four months after the onset of symptoms. However, the lack of antibodies in the blood does not mean antibodies will not be rapidly produced upon reexposure to SARS-CoV-2. Memory B cells specific for the spike and nucleocapsid proteins of SARS-CoV-2 last for at least six months after the appearance of symptoms.[^cite_note-CohenJI2020Dec-331]

As of August 2021, reinfection with COVID‑19 was possible but uncommon. The first case of reinfection was documented in August 2020.[^cite_note-Wang-2021-332] A systematic review found 17 cases of confirmed reinfection in medical literature as of May 2021.[^cite_note-Wang-2021-332] With the Omicron variant, as of 2022, reinfections have become common, albeit it is unclear how common.[^cite_note-abc-reinfections-333] [COVID-19 reinfections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2#Reinfection) are thought to likely be less severe than primary infections, especially if one was previously infected by the same variant.[^cite_note-abc-reinfections-333]

## Mortality

Main articles: [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) and [COVID-19 pandemic death rates by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_death_rates_by_country)

Several measures are commonly used to quantify mortality.[^cite_note-334] These numbers vary by region and over time and are influenced by the volume of testing, healthcare system quality, treatment options, time since the initial outbreak, and population characteristics such as age, sex, and overall health.[^cite_note-\:0-335]

The [mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate) reflects the number of deaths within a specific demographic group divided by the population of that demographic group. Consequently, the mortality rate reflects the prevalence as well as the severity of the disease within a given population. Mortality rates are highly correlated to age, with relatively low rates for young people and relatively high rates among the elderly.[^cite_note-JAMAPedsCOVID19-336][^cite_note-Lu_Zhang_Du_Zhang_p.-337][^cite_note-pediatrics_tong-338] In fact, one relevant factor of mortality rates is the age structure of the countries' populations. For example, the case fatality rate for COVID‑19 is lower in India than in the US since India's younger population represents a larger percentage than in the US.[^cite_note-Dehingia-2021-339]

### Case fatality rate

The [case fatality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_fatality_rate) (CFR) reflects the number of deaths divided by the number of diagnosed cases within a given time interval. Based on Johns Hopkins University statistics, the global death-to-case ratio is 1.02% (6,881,955/676,609,955) as of 10 March 2023.[^cite_note-JHU_ticker-340] The number varies by region.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-\:0-335]

- Total confirmed cases over time

  Total confirmed cases over time

- Total confirmed cases of COVID‑19 per million people

  Total confirmed cases of COVID‑19 per million people

  [^cite_note-342]

- Total confirmed deaths over time

  Total confirmed deaths over time

- Total confirmed deaths due to COVID‑19 per million people

  Total confirmed deaths due to COVID‑19 per million people

  [^cite_note-343]

### Infection fatality rate

A key metric in gauging the severity of COVID‑19 is the [infection fatality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection_fatality_rate) (IFR), also referred to as the *infection fatality ratio* or *infection fatality risk*.[^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346] This metric is calculated by dividing the total number of deaths from the disease by the total number of infected individuals; hence, in contrast to the [CFR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_fatality_rate), the IFR incorporates asymptomatic and undiagnosed infections as well as reported cases.[^cite_note-urlGeneralized_trapezoidal_ogive_curves_for_fatality_rate_modeling-347]

#### Estimates



The red line shows the estimate of infection fatality rate (IFR), in percentage terms, as a function of age. The shaded region depicts the 95% confidence interval for that estimate. Markers denotes specific observations used in the meta-analysis.

[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-348]



The same relationship plotted on a log scale

A December 2020 systematic review and meta-analysis estimated that population IFR during the first wave of the pandemic was about 0.5% to 1% in many locations (including France, Netherlands, New Zealand, and Portugal), 1% to 2% in other locations (Australia, England, Lithuania, and Spain), and exceeded 2% in Italy.[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-348] That study also found that most of these differences in IFR reflected corresponding differences in the age composition of the population and age-specific infection rates; in particular, the metaregression estimate of IFR is extremely low for children and younger adults (e.g., 0.002% at age 10 and 0.01% at age 25) but increases progressively to 0.4% at age 55, 1.4% at age 65, 4.6% at age 75, and 15% at age 85.[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-348] These results were also highlighted in a December 2020 report issued by the WHO.[^cite_note-349]

| Age group | IFR |
| --- | --- |
| 0–34 | 0.004% |
| 35–44 | 0.068% |
| 45–54 | 0.23% |
| 55–64 | 0.75% |
| 65–74 | 2.5% |
| 75–84 | 8.5% |
| 85 + | 28.3% |

An analysis of those IFR rates indicates that COVID‑19 is hazardous not only for the elderly but also for middle-aged adults, for whom the infection fatality rate of COVID-19 is two orders of magnitude greater than the annualised risk of a fatal automobile accident and far more dangerous than seasonal [influenza](19572217.md).[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-348]

#### Earlier estimates of IFR

At an early stage of the pandemic, the World Health Organization reported estimates of IFR between 0.3% and 1%.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] On 2 July, The WHO's chief scientist reported that the average IFR estimate presented at a two-day WHO expert forum was about 0.6%.[^cite_note-NYT-20200704dm-352][^cite_note-353] In August, the WHO found that studies incorporating data from broad serology testing in Europe showed IFR estimates converging at approximately 0.5–1%.[^cite_note-354] Firm lower limits of IFRs have been established in a number of locations such as New York City and Bergamo in Italy since the IFR cannot be less than the population fatality rate. (After sufficient time however, people can get reinfected).[^cite_note-355] As of 10 July, in New York City, with a population of 8.4 million, 23,377 individuals (18,758 confirmed and 4,619 probable) have died with COVID‑19 (0.3% of the population).[^cite_note-356] Antibody testing in New York City suggested an IFR of ≈0.9%,[^cite_note-357] and ≈1.4%.[^cite_note-358] In [Bergamo province](987207.md), 0.6% of the population has died.[^cite_note-359] In September 2020, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported preliminary estimates of age-specific IFRs for public health planning purposes.[^cite_note-\:1-360]

COVID‑19 [case fatality rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_fatality_rate) are higher among men than women in most countries. However, in a few countries like India, Nepal, Vietnam, and Slovenia the fatality cases are higher in women than men.[^cite_note-Dehingia-2021-339] Globally, men are more likely to be admitted to the [ICU](6332859.md) and more likely to die.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363] One meta-analysis found that globally, men were more likely to get COVID‑19 than women; there were approximately 55 men and 45 women per 100 infections ([CI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_interval): 51.43–56.58).[^cite_note-364]

The [Chinese Center for Disease Control and Prevention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Center_for_Disease_Control_and_Prevention) reported the death rate was 2.8% for men and 1.7% for women.[^cite_note-Epidemiology17Feb2020-365] Later reviews in June 2020 indicated that there is no significant difference in susceptibility or in CFR between genders.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] One review acknowledges the different mortality rates in Chinese men, suggesting that it may be attributable to lifestyle choices such as smoking and drinking alcohol rather than genetic factors.[^cite_note-368] Smoking, which in some countries like China is mainly a male activity, is a habit that contributes to increasing significantly the case fatality rates among men.[^cite_note-Dehingia-2021-339] Sex-based immunological differences, lesser prevalence of smoking in women and men developing co-morbid conditions such as hypertension at a younger age than women could have contributed to the higher mortality in men.[^cite_note-nyt-italy-369] In Europe as of February 2020, 57% of the infected people were men and 72% of those died with COVID‑19 were men.[^cite_note-370] Research has shown that viral illnesses like Ebola, HIV, influenza and SARS affect men and women differently.[^cite_note-nytimesus-371]

### Ethnic differences

In the US, a greater proportion of deaths due to COVID‑19 have occurred among African Americans and other minority groups.[^cite_note-AVD-372] Structural factors that prevent them from practising social distancing include their concentration in crowded substandard housing and in "essential" occupations such as retail grocery workers, public transit employees, health-care workers and custodial staff. Greater prevalence of lacking [health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance) and care of underlying conditions such as [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes),[^cite_note-Shauly-Aharonov-2021-373] hypertension, and [heart disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_disease) also increase their risk of death.[^cite_note-374] Similar issues affect [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) and [Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latino_\(demonym\)) communities.[^cite_note-AVD-372] On the one hand, in the Dominican Republic there is a clear example of both gender and ethnic inequality. In this Latin American territory, there is great inequality and precariousness that especially affects Dominican women, with greater emphasis on those of Haitian descent.[^cite_note-Batthyany-2020-375] According to a US health policy non-profit, 34% of American Indian and Alaska Native People (AIAN) non-elderly adults are at risk of serious illness compared to 21% of white non-elderly adults.[^cite_note-\:2-376] The source attributes it to disproportionately high rates of many health conditions that may put them at higher risk as well as living conditions like lack of access to clean water.[^cite_note-\:2-376]

Leaders have called for efforts to research and address the disparities.[^cite_note-377] In the UK, a greater proportion of deaths due to COVID‑19 have occurred in those of a [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_British_people), [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Asian), and other ethnic minority background.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] More severe impacts upon patients including the relative incidence of the necessity of hospitalisation requirements, and vulnerability to the disease has been associated via DNA analysis to be expressed in genetic variants at chromosomal region 3, features that are associated with European [Neanderthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal) heritage. That structure imposes greater risks that those affected will develop a more severe form of the disease.[^cite_note-Neanderthal-381] The findings are from Professor Svante Pääbo and researchers he leads at the [Max Planck Institute for Evolutionary Anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Planck_Institute_for_Evolutionary_Anthropology) and the [Karolinska Institutet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karolinska_Institutet).[^cite_note-Neanderthal-381] This admixture of modern human and Neanderthal genes is estimated to have occurred roughly between 50,000 and 60,000 years ago in Southern Europe.[^cite_note-Neanderthal-381]

### Comorbidities

[Biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology) factors (immune response) and the general behaviour (habits) can strongly determine the consequences of COVID‑19.[^cite_note-Dehingia-2021-339] Most of those who die of COVID‑19 have [pre-existing (underlying) conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-existing_condition), including hypertension, [diabetes mellitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes_mellitus),[^cite_note-Shauly-Aharonov-2021-373] and [cardiovascular disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease).[^cite_note-WHO-2020a-382] According to March data from the United States, 89% of those hospitalised had preexisting conditions.[^cite_note-383] The Italian Istituto Superiore di Sanità reported that out of 8.8% of deaths where [medical charts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_record) were available, 96.1% of people had at least one [comorbidity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comorbidity) with the average person having 3.4 diseases.[^cite_note-ISSCharacteristics-281] According to this report the most common comorbidities are hypertension (66% of deaths), [type 2 diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_2_diabetes) (29.8% of deaths), [ischaemic heart disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ischemic_heart_disease) (27.6% of deaths), [atrial fibrillation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atrial_fibrillation) (23.1% of deaths) and [chronic renal failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_renal_failure) (20.2% of deaths).

Most critical respiratory comorbidities according to the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), are: moderate or severe [asthma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asthma), pre-existing [COPD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_obstructive_pulmonary_disease), [pulmonary fibrosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulmonary_fibrosis), and [cystic fibrosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cystic_fibrosis).[^cite_note-\:3-384] Evidence stemming from [meta-analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meta-analysis) of several smaller research papers also suggests that smoking can be associated with worse outcomes.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386] When someone with existing respiratory problems is infected with COVID‑19, they might be at greater risk for severe symptoms.[^cite_note-\:3-384] COVID‑19 also poses a greater risk to people who [misuse opioids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_use_disorder) and [amphetamines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulant_use_disorder), insofar as their drug use may have caused lung damage.[^cite_note-387]

In August 2020, the CDC issued a caution that [tuberculosis](30653.md) (TB) infections could increase the risk of severe illness or death. The WHO recommended that people with respiratory symptoms be screened for both diseases, as testing positive for COVID‑19 could not rule out co-infections. Some projections have estimated that reduced TB detection due to the pandemic could result in 6.3 million additional TB cases and 1.4 million TB-related deaths by 2025.[^cite_note-388]

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:COVID-19_pandemic_sidebar)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:COVID-19_pandemic_sidebar)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:COVID-19_pandemic_sidebar) |

The virus is thought to be of natural animal origin, most likely through [spillover infection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spillover_infection).[^cite_note-NM-20200317-102][^cite_note-PMC7969828-389][^cite_note-critical-390] A joint-study conducted in early 2021 by the People's Republic of China and the World Health Organization indicated that the virus descended from a coronavirus that infects wild bats, and likely spread to humans through an intermediary wildlife host.[^cite_note-391] There are several theories about where the [index case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_case) originated and [investigations into the origin of the pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investigations_into_the_origin_of_COVID-19) are ongoing.[^cite_note-patientZero-392] According to articles published in July 2022 in *[Science](193513.md)*, virus transmission into humans occurred through two spillover events in November 2019 and was likely due to live wildlife trade on the [Huanan wet market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huanan_Seafood_Wholesale_Market) in the city of [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan) (Hubei, China).[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] Doubts about the conclusions have mostly centered on the precise site of spillover.[^cite_note-396] Earlier [phylogenetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetics) estimated that SARS-CoV-2 arose in October or November 2019.[^cite_note-NM-20200317-102][^cite_note-evolutionary-397][^cite_note-dorp_evo-398] A phylogenetic algorithm analysis suggested that the virus may have been circulating in [Guangdong](65109.md) before Wuhan.[^cite_note-399]

Most scientists believe the virus spilled into human populations through natural [zoonosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoonosis), similar to the [SARS-CoV-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-1) and [MERS-CoV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MERS-CoV) outbreaks, and consistent with other pandemics in human history.[^cite_note-pekar-20][^cite_note-jiang_wang-21] According to the [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](15030.md) several social and environmental factors including [climate change](5042951.md), [natural ecosystem destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_degradation) and [wildlife trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_trade_and_zoonoses) increased the likelihood of such zoonotic spillover.[^cite_note-IPCC-2022a-22][^cite_note-IPCC-2022b-23] One study made with the support of the [European Union](9317.md) found climate change increased the likelihood of the pandemic by influencing distribution of bat species.[^cite_note-University-of-Cambridge-2021-24][^cite_note-European-Commission-25]

Available evidence suggests that the SARS-CoV-2 virus was originally harboured by bats, and spread to humans multiple times from infected wild animals at the [Huanan Seafood Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huanan_Seafood_Market) in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan) in December 2019.[^cite_note-pekar-20][^cite_note-jiang_wang-21] A minority of scientists and some members of the [U.S intelligence community](501244.md) believe the virus may have been [unintentionally leaked](66692273.md) from a laboratory such as the [Wuhan Institute of Virology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan_Institute_of_Virology).[^cite_note-NYT_DoE_Feb2023-400][^cite_note-Hill_Feb_2023_DoE-401] The US intelligence community has mixed views on the issue,[^cite_note-CNN_Feb2023_LeBlanc-402][^cite_note-Guardian_Feb2023-403] but overall agrees with the scientific consensus that the virus was not developed as a [biological weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_agent) and is unlikely to have been [genetically engineered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_engineering).[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] There is no evidence SARS-CoV-2 existed in any laboratory prior to the pandemic.[^cite_note-critical-390][^cite_note-Gorski-408][^cite_note-HolmesConversationDead-409]

The first confirmed human infections were in Wuhan. A study of the first 41 cases of confirmed COVID‑19, published in January 2020 in *The Lancet*, reported the earliest date of onset of symptoms as 1 December 2019.[^cite_note-WuMarch2020-410][^cite_note-Wang24Jan2020-411][^cite_note-AutoDW-67-412] Official publications from the WHO reported the earliest onset of symptoms as 8 December 2019.[^cite_note-413] Human-to-human transmission was confirmed by the WHO and Chinese authorities by 20 January 2020.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415] According to official Chinese sources, these were mostly linked to the [Huanan Seafood Wholesale Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huanan_Seafood_Wholesale_Market), which also sold live animals.[^cite_note-characteristicsZH-416] In May 2020, [George Gao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_F._Gao), the director of the CDC, said animal samples collected from the seafood market had tested negative for the virus, indicating that the market was the site of an early [superspreading event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superspreader), but that it was not the site of the initial outbreak.[^cite_note-417] Traces of the virus have been found in wastewater samples that were collected in [Milan](36511.md) and [Turin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turin), Italy, on 18 December 2019.[^cite_note-418]

By December 2019, the spread of infection was almost entirely driven by human-to-human transmission.[^cite_note-Epidemiology17Feb2020-365][^cite_note-419] The number of COVID-19 cases in Hubei gradually increased, reaching 60 by 20 December,[^cite_note-420] and at least 266 by 31 December.[^cite_note-421] On 24 December, [Wuhan Central Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan_Central_Hospital) sent a [bronchoalveolar lavage fluid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronchoalveolar_lavage_fluid) (BAL) sample from an unresolved clinical case to sequencing company Vision Medicals. On 27 and 28 December, Vision Medicals informed the Wuhan Central Hospital and the Chinese CDC of the results of the test, showing a new coronavirus.[^cite_note-422] A pneumonia cluster of unknown cause was observed on 26 December and treated by the doctor [Zhang Jixian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Jixian) in Hubei Provincial Hospital, who informed the Wuhan Jianghan CDC on 27 December.[^cite_note-423] On 30 December, a test report addressed to Wuhan Central Hospital, from company CapitalBio Medlab, stated an erroneous positive result for [SARS](196741.md), causing a group of doctors at Wuhan Central Hospital to alert their colleagues and relevant hospital authorities of the result. The Wuhan Municipal Health Commission issued a notice to various medical institutions on "the treatment of pneumonia of unknown cause" that same evening.[^cite_note-424] Eight of these doctors, including [Li Wenliang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Wenliang) (punished on 3 January),[^cite_note-425] were later admonished by the police for spreading false rumours and another, [Ai Fen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ai_Fen), was reprimanded by her superiors for raising the alarm.[^cite_note-426]

The Wuhan Municipal Health Commission made the first public announcement of a pneumonia outbreak of unknown cause on 31 December, confirming 27 cases[^cite_note-AutoDW-69-427][^cite_note-428][^cite_note-AutoDW-68-429] – enough to trigger an investigation.[^cite_note-bbc50984025-430]

During the early stages of the outbreak, the number of cases doubled approximately every seven and a half days.[^cite_note-Qun29Jan2020-431] In early and mid-January 2020, the virus spread to other [Chinese provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_China), helped by the [Chinese New Year migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chunyun) and Wuhan being a transport hub and major rail interchange.[^cite_note-WHOReport24Feb2020-107] On 20 January, China reported nearly 140 new cases in one day, including two people in Beijing and one in [Shenzhen](83368.md).[^cite_note-france2420200120-432] Later official data shows 6,174 people had already developed symptoms by then,[^cite_note-Epidemiology17Feb2020-365] and more may have been infected.[^cite_note-flattery-433] A report in *The Lancet* on 24 January indicated human transmission, strongly recommended personal protective equipment for health workers, and said testing for the virus was essential due to its "pandemic potential".[^cite_note-Huang24Jan2020-136][^cite_note-Horton_18_March-434] On 30 January, the WHO declared COVID-19 a [Public Health Emergency of International Concern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_Emergency_of_International_Concern).[^cite_note-flattery-433] By this time, the outbreak spread by a factor of 100 to 200 times.[^cite_note-435]

Italy had its first confirmed cases on 31 January 2020, two tourists from China.[^cite_note-Corriere_20Jan-436] Italy overtook China as the country with the most deaths on 19 March 2020.[^cite_note-sky11960412-437] By 26 March the United States had overtaken China and Italy with the highest number of confirmed cases in the world.[^cite_note-NYT-20200326-438] Research on coronavirus genomes indicates the majority of COVID-19 cases in [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_New_York_\(state\)) came from European travellers, rather than directly from China or any other Asian country.[^cite_note-20200408nytimes-439] Retesting of prior samples found a person in France who had the virus on 27 December 2019,[^cite_note-France-retest-440][^cite_note-Deslandes-2020-441] and a person in the United States who died from the disease on 6 February 2020.[^cite_note-PBS-2wks-442]

RT-PCR testing of untreated wastewater samples from Brazil and Italy have suggested detection of SARS-CoV-2 as early as November and December 2019, respectively, but the methods of such sewage studies have not been optimised, many have not been peer-reviewed, details are often missing, and there is a risk of false positives due to contamination or if only one gene target is detected.[^cite_note-443] A September 2020 review journal article said, "The possibility that the COVID‑19 infection had already spread to Europe at the end of last year is now indicated by abundant, even if partially circumstantial, evidence", including pneumonia case numbers and radiology in France and Italy in November and December.[^cite_note-platto-444]

As of 1 October 2021, *[Reuters](18998750.md)* reported that it had estimated the worldwide total number of deaths due to COVID‑19 to have exceeded five million.[^cite_note-445]

The Public Health Emergency of International Concern for COVID-19 ended on 5 May 2023. By this time, everyday life in most countries had returned to how it was before the pandemic.[^cite_note-446][^cite_note-447]

## Misinformation

Main article: [COVID-19 misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation)

After the initial outbreak of COVID‑19, [misinformation](2203174.md) and [disinformation](142690.md) regarding the origin, scale, prevention, treatment, and other aspects of the disease rapidly spread online.[^cite_note-bbc_misinfo-448][^cite_note-GUAR-449][^cite_note-RunningList-450]

## Other species

See also: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_animals)

Humans appear to be capable of spreading the virus to some other animals,[^cite_note-Gryseels_2021_MammalReview-451][^cite_note-TanLam2022_NatComm-452] a type of disease transmission referred to as [zooanthroponosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthroponotic_disease).[^cite_note-Pappas_MDPI_2022-453][^cite_note-MunirAshraf_2020-454]

Some pets, especially cats and [ferrets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferret), can catch this virus from infected humans.[^cite_note-Kampf-2020-455][^cite_note-456] Symptoms in cats include [respiratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_symptom) (such as a cough) and digestive symptoms.[^cite_note-Kampf-2020-455] Cats can spread the virus to other cats, and may be able to spread the virus to humans, but cat-to-human transmission of SARS-CoV-2 has not been proven.[^cite_note-Kampf-2020-455][^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457] Compared to cats, dogs are less susceptible to this infection.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457] Behaviours which increase the risk of transmission include kissing, licking, and petting the animal.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457] Rabbits can also be infected with Covid-19.[^cite_note-458]

The virus does not appear to be able to infect [pigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pig), [ducks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_duck), or chickens at all.[^cite_note-Kampf-2020-455] [Mice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mouse), rats, and rabbits are unlikely to be involved in spreading the virus.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457]

Tigers and lions in zoos have become infected as a result of contact with infected humans.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457] As expected, monkeys and [great ape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_ape) species such as [orangutans](22433.md) can also be infected with the COVID‑19 virus.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457]

[Minks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mink), which are in the [same family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weasel_family) as ferrets, have been infected.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457] Minks may be asymptomatic, and can also spread the virus to humans.[^cite_note-Tazerji-2020-457] Multiple countries have identified infected animals in [mink farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mink_farm).[^cite_note-Gorman-2021-459] [Denmark](76972.md), a major producer of mink pelts, ordered the slaughter of all minks over fears of viral mutations,[^cite_note-Gorman-2021-459] following an outbreak referred to as [Cluster 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluster_5). A vaccine for mink and other animals was being researched in 2021, though by 2025 it appeared that animals were effectively vaccinated, with the exception of polar bears.[^cite_note-Gorman-2021-459][^cite_note-460]

## Research

Further information: [COVID-19 drug development](63435931.md)

International research on vaccines and medicines in COVID‑19 is underway by government organisations, academic groups, and industry researchers.[^cite_note-dhama-461][^cite_note-zhang2020-462] There has been a great deal of COVID‑19 research, involving accelerated research processes and publishing shortcuts to meet the global demand.[^cite_note-Aristovnik_et_al._2020-463] By the end of 2020, hundreds of [clinical trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinical_trial) had been undertaken, with research happening on every continent except [Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Antarctica).[^cite_note-464] More than 200 possible treatment were tested in humans that first year.[^cite_note-465]

### Transmission and prevention research

Further information: [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md)

[Modelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modeling_and_simulation) research has been conducted with several objectives, including predictions of the dynamics of transmission,[^cite_note-466] diagnosis and prognosis of infection,[^cite_note-467] estimation of the impact of interventions,[^cite_note-468][^cite_note-469] or allocation of resources.[^cite_note-470] Modelling studies are mostly based on [compartmental models in epidemiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compartmental_models_in_epidemiology),[^cite_note-471] estimating the number of infected people over time under given conditions. Several other types of models have been developed and used during the COVID‑19 pandemic including [computational fluid dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_fluid_dynamics) models to study the flow physics of COVID‑19,[^cite_note-472] retrofits of crowd movement models to study occupant exposure,[^cite_note-473] mobility-data based models to investigate transmission,[^cite_note-474] or the use of [macroeconomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macroeconomic) models to assess the economic impact of the pandemic.[^cite_note-475]

### Treatment-related research

Main article: [COVID-19 drug repurposing research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_drug_repurposing_research)



Seven possible drug targets in viral replication process and drugs

Repurposed [antiviral drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiviral_drug) make up most of the research into COVID‑19 treatments.[^cite_note-milken-476][^cite_note-koch-477] Other candidates in trials include [vasodilators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasodilator), [corticosteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corticosteroid), immune therapies, [lipoic acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lipoic_acid), [bevacizumab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bevacizumab), and [recombinant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recombinant_DNA) angiotensin-converting enzyme 2.[^cite_note-koch-477]

In March 2020, the World Health Organization (WHO) initiated the [Solidarity trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_trial) to assess the treatment effects of some promising drugs:[^cite_note-kai-478][^cite_note-479]

- An [experimental drug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_drug) called [remdesivir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remdesivir)

- [Anti-malarial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antimalarial_medication) drugs [chloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chloroquine) and [hydroxychloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxychloroquine)

- Two [anti-HIV drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-HIV_medications), [lopinavir/ritonavir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lopinavir/ritonavir) and [interferon-beta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interferon-beta)

Research on the antimalarial drugs hydroxychloroquine and chloroquine showed that they were ineffective at best,[^cite_note-20200526nytimes-480][^cite_note-481] and that they may reduce the antiviral activity of remdesivir.[^cite_note-482]

In June, initial results from the randomised [RECOVERY Trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RECOVERY_Trial) in the United Kingdom showed that [dexamethasone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dexamethasone) reduced mortality by one third for people who are critically ill on ventilators and one fifth for those receiving supplemental oxygen.[^cite_note-483] Because this is a well-tested and widely available treatment, it was welcomed by the WHO, which is in the process of updating treatment guidelines to include dexamethasone and other steroids.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485] Based on those preliminary results, dexamethasone treatment has been recommended by the NIH for peoples with COVID‑19 who are mechanically ventilated or who require supplemental oxygen but not in people with COVID‑19 who do not require supplemental oxygen.[^cite_note-486]

In September 2020, the WHO released updated guidance on using corticosteroids for COVID‑19.[^cite_note-WHO_guidance-487][^cite_note-488] The WHO recommends systemic corticosteroids for the treatment of people with severe and critical COVID‑19.[^cite_note-WHO_guidance-487] The WHO suggests not to use corticosteroids in the treatment of people with non-severe COVID‑19.[^cite_note-WHO_guidance-487] The updated guidance was based on a meta-analysis of clinical trials of people critically ill with COVID‑19.[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-490]

In November 2020, the US [Food and Drug Administration](11632.md) (FDA) issued an emergency use authorisation for the investigational monoclonal antibody therapy [bamlanivimab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamlanivimab) for the treatment of mild-to-moderate COVID‑19.[^cite_note-FDA_bamlanivimab_EUA-491] Bamlanivimab is authorised for people with positive results of direct SARS-CoV-2 viral testing who are twelve years of age and older weighing at least 40 kilograms (88 lb), and who are at high risk for progressing to severe COVID‑19 or hospitalisation.[^cite_note-FDA_bamlanivimab_EUA-491] This includes those who are 65 years of age or older, or who have chronic medical conditions.[^cite_note-FDA_bamlanivimab_EUA-491]

In February 2021, the FDA issued an emergency use authorisation (EUA) for bamlanivimab and [etesevimab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etesevimab) administered together for the treatment of mild to moderate COVID‑19 in people twelve years of age or older weighing at least 40 kilograms (88 lb) who test positive for SARS‑CoV‑2 and who are at high risk for progressing to severe COVID‑19. The authorised use includes treatment for those who are 65 years of age or older or who have certain chronic medical conditions.[^cite_note-FDA_PR_20210209-492]

In April 2021, the FDA revoked the emergency use authorisation (EUA) that allowed for the investigational monoclonal antibody therapy bamlanivimab, when administered alone, to be used for the treatment of mild-to-moderate COVID‑19 in adults and certain paediatric patients.[^cite_note-FDA_PR_20210416-493]

#### Cytokine storm



Various therapeutic strategies for targeting cytokine storm

A [cytokine storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytokine_storm) can be a complication in the later stages of severe COVID‑19. A cytokine storm is a potentially deadly immune reaction where a large amount of pro-inflammatory cytokines and [chemokines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemokine) are released too quickly. A cytokine storm can lead to ARDS and multiple organ failure.[^cite_note-494] Data collected from Jin Yin-tan Hospital in Wuhan, China indicates that people who had more severe responses to COVID‑19 had greater amounts of pro-inflammatory cytokines and chemokines in their system than people who had milder responses. These high levels of pro-inflammatory cytokines and chemokines indicate presence of a cytokine storm.[^cite_note-495]

[Tocilizumab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocilizumab) has been included in treatment guidelines by China's [National Health Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Commission) after a small study was completed.[^cite_note-tocil-1-496][^cite_note-tocil-2-497] It is undergoing a [Phase II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phase_IIb) non-randomised trial at the national level in Italy after showing positive results in people with severe disease.[^cite_note-498][^cite_note-499] Combined with a serum [ferritin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrite_\(magnet\)) blood test to identify a cytokine storm (also called cytokine storm syndrome, not to be confused with [cytokine release syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytokine_release_syndrome)), it is meant to counter such developments, which are thought to be the cause of death in some affected people.[^cite_note-tocil-5,6,8-500] The [interleukin-6 receptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interleukin-6_receptor) (IL-6R) [antagonist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Receptor_antagonist) was approved by the FDA to undergo a Phase III clinical trial assessing its effectiveness on COVID‑19 based on retrospective case studies for the treatment of steroid-refractory cytokine release syndrome induced by a different cause, [CAR T cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chimeric_antigen_receptor_T_cell) [therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_therapy), in 2017.[^cite_note-CancerNetworkTocilizumabTrial-501] There is no randomised, controlled evidence that tocilizumab is an efficacious treatment for CRS. Prophylactic tocilizumab has been shown to increase serum IL-6 levels by saturating the IL-6R, driving IL-6 across the [blood–brain barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood%E2%80%93brain_barrier), and exacerbating [neurotoxicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurotoxicity) while having no effect on the incidence of CRS.[^cite_note-502]

[Lenzilumab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenzilumab), an anti-GM-CSF [monoclonal antibody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoclonal_antibody), is protective in murine models for CAR T cell-induced CRS and neurotoxicity and is a viable therapeutic option due to the observed increase of pathogenic GM-CSF secreting T cells in hospitalised patients with COVID‑19.[^cite_note-503]

#### Passive antibodies



Overview of the application and use of [convalescent plasma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convalescent_plasma) therapy

Transferring purified and concentrated [antibodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunoglobulin_therapy) produced by the immune systems of those who have recovered from COVID‑19 to people who need them is being investigated as a non-vaccine method of [passive immunisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passive_immunity).[^cite_note-pmid-32167489-504][^cite_note-Iannizzi-2023-505] [Viral neutralisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutralisation_\(immunology\)) is the anticipated [mechanism of action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanism_of_action) by which passive antibody therapy can mediate defence against SARS-CoV-2. The spike protein of SARS-CoV-2 is the primary target for neutralising antibodies.[^cite_note-Ho-2020-506] As of 8 August 2020, eight neutralising antibodies targeting the spike protein of SARS-CoV-2 have entered clinical studies.[^cite_note-507] It has been proposed that selection of broad-neutralising antibodies against SARS-CoV-2 and SARS-CoV might be useful for treating not only COVID‑19 but also future SARS-related CoV infections.[^cite_note-Ho-2020-506] Other mechanisms, however, such as [antibody-dependant cellular cytotoxicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibody-dependent_cellular_cytotoxicity) or [phagocytosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phagocytosis), may be possible.[^cite_note-pmid-32167489-504] Other forms of passive antibody therapy, for example, using manufactured monoclonal antibodies, are in development.[^cite_note-pmid-32167489-504]

The use of passive antibodies to treat people with active COVID‑19 is also being studied. This involves the production of [convalescent serum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convalescent_serum), which consists of the liquid portion of the blood from people who recovered from the infection and contains antibodies specific to this virus, which is then administered to active patients.[^cite_note-pmid-32167489-504] This strategy was tried for SARS with inconclusive results.[^cite_note-pmid-32167489-504] An updated Cochrane review in May 2023 found high certainty evidence that, for the treatment of people with moderate to severe COVID‑19, convalescent plasma did not reduce mortality or bring about symptom improvement.[^cite_note-Iannizzi-2023-505] There continues to be uncertainty about the safety of convalescent plasma administration to people with COVID‑19 and differing outcomes measured in different studies limits their use in determining efficacy.[^cite_note-Iannizzi-2023-505]

### Bioethics

Since the outbreak of the COVID‑19 pandemic, scholars have explored the [bioethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioethics), [normative economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normative_economics), and [political theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_philosophy) of [healthcare policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_policy) related to the public health crisis.[^cite_note-508] Academics have pointed to the moral distress of healthcare workers, ethics of distributing scarce healthcare resources such as ventilators,[^cite_note-509] and the global justice of vaccine diplomacies.[^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511] The socio-economic inequalities between genders,[^cite_note-512] races,[^cite_note-513] groups with disabilities,[^cite_note-514] communities,[^cite_note-515] regions, countries,[^cite_note-516] and continents have also drawn attention in academia and the general public.[^cite_note-517][^cite_note-518]

[Nature Portfolio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_Portfolio). [Bibcode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibcode_\(identifier\)):[2021NatSR..1115998C](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2021NatSR..1115998C). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1038/s41598-021-94960-5](https://doi.org/10.1038%2Fs41598-021-94960-5). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [8346500](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8346500). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [34362936](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34362936). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [236947786](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:236947786). Masks prevent the spread of droplets and aerosols generated by an infected individual, and when correctly worn surgical masks can reduce viral transmission by 95%. Uninfected individuals wearing a surgical mask are about 85% protected against infection.

[^cite_note-Face_masks_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic_Talic-215]: &#x20;Talic S, Shah S, Wild H, Gasevic D, Maharaj A, Ademi Z, et al. (November 2021). ["Effectiveness of public health measures in reducing the incidence of covid-19, SARS-CoV-2 transmission, and covid-19 mortality: systematic review and meta-analysis"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9423125). *BMJ*. **375** e068302. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1136/bmj-2021-068302](https://doi.org/10.1136%2Fbmj-2021-068302). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [9423125](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9423125). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [34789505](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34789505). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [244271780](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:244271780). The results of additional studies that assessed mask wearing ... indicate a reduction in covid-19 incidence, SARS-CoV-2 transmission, and covid-19 mortality. Specifically, a natural experiment across 200 countries showed 45.7% fewer covid-19 related mortality in countries where mask-wearing was mandatory. Another natural experiment study in the US reported a 29% reduction in SARS-CoV-2 transmission (measured as the time-varying reproductive number Rt) (risk ratio 0.71, 95% confidence interval 0.58 to 0.75) in states where mask-wearing was mandatory. A comparative study in the Hong Kong Special Administrative Region reported a statistically significantly lower cumulative incidence of covid-19 associated with mask-wearing than in selected countries where mask-wearing was not mandatory.

[^cite_note-Face_masks_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic_cdc-sci-216]: &#x20;["Science Brief: Community Use of Masks to Control the Spread of SARS-CoV-2"](https://www.cdc.gov/coronavirus/2019-ncov/science/science-briefs/masking-science-sars-cov2.html). CDC. 11 February 2020. Experimental and epidemiologic data support community masking to reduce the spread of SARS-CoV-2, including alpha and delta variants, among adults and children. \[...] Mask use has been found to be safe and is not associated with clinically significant impacts on respiration or gas exchange under most circumstances, except for intense exercise. The limited available data indicate no clear evidence that masking impairs emotional or language development in children.*&#x20;*\[I]n combination with other contextual cues, masks are unlikely to produce serious impairments of children's social interactions. A study of 2-year-old children concluded that they were able to recognize familiar words presented without a mask and when hearing words through opaque masks. Among children with autism spectrum disorders (ASD), interventions including positive reinforcement and coaching caregivers to teach mask-wearing have improved participants' ability to wear a face mask. These findings suggest that even children who may have difficulty wearing a mask can do so effectively through targeted interventions.

[^cite_note-HolmesConversationDead-409]: &#x20;Holmes EC (14 August 2022). ["The COVID lab leak theory is dead. Here's how we know the virus came from a Wuhan market"](https://theconversation.com/the-covid-lab-leak-theory-is-dead-heres-how-we-know-the-virus-came-from-a-wuhan-market-188163). *The Conversation*. Retrieved 4 September 2022. For the lab leak theory to be true, SARS-CoV-2 must have been present in the Wuhan Institute of Virology before the pandemic started. This would convince me. But the inconvenient truth is there's not a single piece of data suggesting this. There's no evidence for a genome sequence or isolate of a precursor virus at the Wuhan Institute of Virology. Not from gene sequence databases, scientific publications, annual reports, student theses, social media, or emails. Even the intelligence community has found nothing. Nothing. And there was no reason to keep any work on a SARS-CoV-2 ancestor secret before the pandemic.
